# Transcripts: `raw-files`

Generated by render.py from raw/*.jsonl and *.meta.json; nothing edited.

45 answers, in question order. The evidence is `raw/<qid>-r<n>.jsonl` (every event, untouched) and `raw/<qid>-r<n>.meta.json` (prompt, system prompt, settings, exact command). Tool calls are cut at 400 characters and results at 500; the final answer is whole. The one rewrite: the home directory of the machine that ran it appears as `<HOME>`.

## Q1-r1

arm `raw`, model `claude-sonnet-5-5`, wall 33.5 s

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Frequency: across all five years, what percentage of cases have base section 16(h), and what percentage have base section 20(a)(2)? Round to one decimal.
```

```
You are a careful analyst. You can use the Read, Grep, Glob and Bash tools (Bash has Python 3). Your working directory is the only place you may look for files; use only files in it. Show the number or answer each question asks for in your final reply.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
Across all five years, **39.6%** of cases have base section 16(h) and **22.9%** have base section 20(a)(2).

- **16(h):** 3,939 of 9,957 cases.
- **20(a)(2):** 2,284 of 9,957 cases.

I read all 58 order text files in the data folder and found 9,963 recommendation pages. 9,957 of them have a readable cite. The other 6 pages have a blank form where the cite should be. They are one page in ord_PSD_022322, one in ord_PSD_030321, one in ord_PSD_040622 and three in ord_PSD_071421. I couldn't recover them because the PDF tools I'd need aren't installed.

I excluded those 6 pages from the denominator. If you counted them as cases, 16(h) would be 39.5% instead (3,939 of 9,963). The 20(a)(2) figure stays 22.9% either way. If the 6 blank pages were counted and their hidden cites turned out to be 16(h), the 16(h) figure would move slightly. The effect is at most a few hundredths of a point.

I also treated a few scan errors as the section they clearly meant, for example "8-l-26" as "8-1-26", "20(a)(l)" as "20(a)(1)" and "lS(h)" as "18(h)".

I counted the nunc pro tunc correction order, ord_PSDNPT_040622, as a case. It is one of the 58 orders and has one recommendation page, which is the blank one noted above.
```

```
Of the 9,957 cases with a readable cite, 39.6% have base section 16(h) and 22.9% have base section 20(a)(2).

| Base section | Cases | Share of cases |
|---|---|---|
| 16(h), including (expire), (prior), (out) and (other) | 3,939 | 39.6% |
| 20(a)(2) | 2,284 | 22.9% |

- **Method:** I read all 58 order texts in `data/text` and took the first IC 8-1-26 cite on each recommendation page. Then I removed parenthesized words of two or more letters. I also corrected OCR errors in the cites, such as "8-l-26" for "8-1-26" and "lS(h)" for "18(h)".
- **Blank forms:** Five pages are empty template forms with no case number or cite. I did not count them as cases.
- **Unreadable cite:** One real case, number 24409 (Cobalt Civil, LLC), has a blank cite in the text. If it is counted in the denominator, the shares don't change at one decimal.
- **Not checked against the PDFs:** I couldn't open the original PDFs here, so these figures rest on the OCR text. That blank cite might be readable in the PDF.

**Answer: 16(h) = 39.6%; 20(a)(2) = 22.9%.**
```

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Frequency: across all five years, what percentage of cases have base section 16(h), and what percentage have base section 20(a)(2)? Round to one decimal.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
Across all five years, **39.6%** of cases have base section 16(h) and **22.9%** have base section 20(a)(2).

- **Counts:** I found 3,939 cases with 16(h) and 2,284 with 20(a)(2), out of 9,957 cases that carry a readable cite. I parsed the cite from the text of all 58 orders.
- **OCR repair:** A few cites were misread in the text. One "IC 8-l-26-16(h)" had a lowercase L for the 1, so I read it as 16(h). Seven "20(a)(l)" cites and one "lS(h)" were not in either count, because they belong to 20(a)(1) and 18(h).
- **Cases with no readable cite:** Cobalt Civil (case 24409) shows a blank cite. Green Veden (case 20170) printed its cite as "8-l-26-16(h)", which I counted. Adding Cobalt would make the base 9,958, which doesn't change either rounded figure.
- **Pages that aren't cases:** I excluded six blank template pages that have no respondent or case number. I also excluded the nunc pro tunc order that corrects the address in case 24633, a case already counted in the February 2022 order.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
2022 has the most cases, with 2,672.

| Year | Orders | Cases |
|---|---|---|
| 2021 | 11 | 1,603 |
| 2022 | 12 | 2,672 |
| 2023 | 14 | 2,107 |
| 2024 | 11 | 1,897 |
| 2025 | 10 | 1,684 |
| **Total** | **58** | **9,963** |

**Method:** In each order's text, page 1 is the order itself. Every later page is one recommendation form, and each of those pages contains the "finds the following violation(s)" line. I counted each order's pages after the first. I took the year from the date in each file name (for example, `ord_PSD_110321` is 11/03/2021). The order text has no printed date, so I couldn't check the file-name dates against the orders themselves.

**Nunc pro tunc order:** The `ord_PSDNPT_040622` order has one form. It corrects the damage address in Case 24633, which the Commission had already approved on 2022-02-23. I counted it as a case in 2022, because it is a recommendation form in a 2022 order. If you count only distinct case numbers, 2022 drops to 2,671. The ranking doesn't change.

**Cross-check:** Counting the "Case Number" labels gave the same totals for every order except two. Both differences come from the text extraction, not from the orders. `ord_PSD_042121` has one form whose "finds the following" line isn't machine-readable. `ord_PSD_110321` has five forms where the line is garbled or the case number is out of order. The page-based count covers all of these.
```

```
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Trend: how many cases are there in each year 2021, 2022, 2023, 2024 and 2025, and which year has the most?
```

```
Cases per year (by order date) are:

| Year | Cases |
|---|---|
| 2021 | 1,603 |
| 2022 | 2,672 |
| 2023 | 2,107 |
| 2024 | 1,897 |
| 2025 | 1,684 |

The total is 9,963 cases across the 58 orders, and **2022 has the most** with 2,672.

- **Method:** I counted the recommendation-form pages in each order's text, skipping the cover page. I took each order's year from its filename date, because the order date isn't in the extracted text.
- **Scan errors:** A few "finds the following violation(s)" lines were garbled by scanning (for example "fmds" and "f~llowing"). That is why I counted case-number pages instead of that phrase.
- **Nunc pro tunc order:** The 4/6/2022 nunc pro tunc order (`ord_PSDNPT_040622`) corrects the address on case 24633 and has one form. I counted it as one 2022 case. If you'd rather exclude it, 2022 drops to 2,671, which doesn't change the answer.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
Mean headline penalty, counting only cases with a headline penalty above $0, across the 58 orders:

| Base section | Cases with penalty > $0 | Mean headline penalty |
|---|---|---|
| 16(h) | 2,501 | **$4,199** (exactly $4,198.68) |
| 20(a)(2) | 1,888 | **$3,787** (exactly $3,787.45) |

**Counting rules**
- A case counts toward a base section once any parenthesized qualifier, such as (expire) or (prior), is dropped. I also read OCR variants like "8-l-26" as "8-1-26".
- I applied your headline-penalty rules: the first dollar amount, with a doubled "$" and spaces after thousands commas ignored.
- A case with no "$" amount counts as $0 and is left out of the means. That covers warning letters, "Training (completed …)" and "no penalty recommended". It also covers "3000" and ".5000", which have no "$" sign.
- I included the nunc pro tunc order in the 58 orders. It has only 3 case forms, so it barely affects the means.

**Where the data could be off**
- The text files are OCR output. Any OCR error in a cite or dollar amount that my patterns didn't catch would shift the results slightly.
- Seven blank template pages with no cite were skipped.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
Mean headline penalty, counting only cases with a headline penalty above $0:

- **Base section 16(h): $4,198.** This covers 2,499 cases and includes the qualified forms such as (expire), (prior), (out) and (other).
- **Base section 20(a)(2): $3,789.** This covers 1,887 cases.

How I got these:
- **Penalty amount:** I read the first dollar amount in each recommendation. "Training or $5,000 if respondent fails to complete training" therefore counts as $5,000, and "Warning letter" counts as $0 and drops out of the mean.
- **Scanned-text errors:** I treated "8-l-26" as "8-1-26", and read "(a)(l)" as "(a)(1)".
- **Blank forms:** Six forms in the text have no respondent or cite, so I excluded them.
- **Checkbox forms:** Six cases in the April 2021 order use an older checkbox layout. In each, the marked box is "Training or $5,000", so I counted them as $5,000. Five are 16(h) and one is 20(a)(2). This is my reading of the scan, so it is an estimate. Without those six, the means are $4,197 and $3,788.
```

## Q3-r3

arm `raw`, model `claude-sonnet-5-5`, wall 64.0 s

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Severity by violation: among cases with a headline penalty above $0, what is the mean headline penalty for base section 16(h), and for base section 20(a)(2)? Round each to the nearest dollar.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
Among cases with a headline penalty above $0, the mean headline penalty is **$4,197 for base section 16(h)** and **$3,787 for base section 20(a)(2)**.

| Base section | Cases with penalty above $0 | Mean headline penalty |
|---|---|---|
| 16(h) | 2,502 | $4,197.40, which rounds to **$4,197** |
| 20(a)(2) | 1,888 | $3,787.45, which rounds to **$3,787** |

I parsed all 58 order text files. Three kinds of pages needed special handling:
- **Blank template pages:** I excluded them. They have no case number or cite (five of them have a "Case Number" label but no cite).
- **Scanned pages:** The OCR misread some cites, for example "8-l-26" for "8-1-26", "20(a)(l)" for "20(a)(1)" and "1S(h)" for "16(h)". I corrected these to the intended section. I counted the one "1S(h)" case, a CenterPoint $1,000 case in `ord_PSD_PSDNPT_040622`, as 16(h).
- **Checkbox forms in `ord_PSD_042121`:** I took the ticked option's first dollar amount. That is $5,000 for the "Training or $5,000" box, which applies to five 16(h) cases and one 20(a)(2) case.

I did not check the 58 orders' dates against the 2021–2025 window; the filenames are all dated 2021–2025.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
Of the 58 orders (9,963 cases), I found:

- **Utility respondents: 0.0%.** 1 of 2,713 cases has "warning letter" in the recommendation, which is 0.037%.
- **Non-utility respondents: 28.0%.** 2,033 of 7,250 cases do, which is 28.04%.

- **Search scope:** I searched only the recommendation text, between the civil-penalty line and "Abstentions". I normalized line breaks and ignored case. Two further cases mention a warning letter only under "Additional Information", and I did not count them.
- **Checkbox forms:** 8 cases in the 4/21/2021 order use an older checkbox form. That form lists "Warning letter" as an option in every case, so a plain text match would count them wrongly. I counted only a checked box, and none of the 8 has it checked. All 8 respondents are non-utility, so they sit in the 7,250 denominator without a warning.
- **Nunc pro tunc order:** The 4/6/22 order corrects one case and I included it as an order. Its one case does not change the result.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
**Utility respondents: 0.0%. Non-utility respondents: 28.1%.**

I parsed the recommendation form on every page of the 58 order texts. That gave 9,963 cases, of which 2,715 are utility respondents and 7,248 are non-utility respondents. I then checked each recommendation for "warning letter" (any capitalization, any spacing).

| Group | Cases | With "warning letter" | Share |
|---|---|---|---|
| Utility respondents | 2,715 | 1 | 0.04%, which rounds to **0.0%** |
| Non-utility respondents | 7,248 | 2,039 | **28.1%** |

- **Blank forms:** I left out the blank template pages that have no case number or citation.
- **Nunc pro tunc order:** I counted its single corrected form as a case.
- **Checkbox-style forms:** In `ord_PSD_042121`, 7 forms list "Warning letter" as an unchecked option. I counted them because the question asks whether the recommendation contains the words. If you count only forms that actually recommend a warning letter, those 7 drop out and the non-utility figure becomes 28.0%. The utility figure stays 0.0%.
- **Jumbled layouts:** On some pages the OCR text puts the respondent name on a different line from its label. I recovered the name from the lines after the label block. One case in `ord_PSD_042121` still came out with no respondent name. It is counted as non-utility, which changes neither percentage at one decimal.
```

## Q4-r3

arm `raw`, model `claude-sonnet-5-5`, wall 89.5 s

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Warnings: what percentage of utility-respondent cases, and what percentage of non-utility-respondent cases, have a recommendation containing the words "warning letter" (any capitalization)? Round to one decimal.
```

```
You are a careful analyst. You can use the Read, Grep, Glob and Bash tools (Bash has Python 3). Your working directory is the only place you may look for files; use only files in it. Show the number or answer each question asks for in your final reply.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
Of the cases I could read, **0.0%** of utility-respondent cases and **28.1%** of non-utility-respondent cases have a recommendation with "warning letter".

- **Utility respondents:** 1 of 2,715 cases, which is 0.04% and rounds to 0.0%.
- **Non-utility respondents:** 2,032 of 7,243 cases, which is 28.05% and rounds to 28.1%.

The 58 orders hold 9,958 real recommendation forms. I ignored the blank template pages and the correction order's duplicate entry.

**Respondent field:** One form (case 28073) has a blank respondent, so by your definition it counts as non-utility. In older scanned forms the respondent sits below the field labels, so I read it from there.

**Old checkbox forms:** Seven scanned forms from the 2021 order (ord_PSD_042121) print "Warning letter" as one option in a checkbox list. In all seven, that box is unchecked and training is checked. I counted them as not warnings.

If you counted every appearance of the words, the non-utility figure would be 2,039 of 7,243, which is 28.2%. The utility figure would stay at 0.0%.
```

## Q5-r1

arm `raw`, model `claude-sonnet-5-5`, wall 21.5 s

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Training option: among cases with a headline penalty above $0, what percentage have a recommendation that contains the word "training" (any capitalization)? Round to one decimal.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
**44.7%** of cases with a headline penalty above $0 have a recommendation containing "training".

- **Cases parsed:** 9,954 recommendation forms across the 58 orders.
- **Headline penalty above $0:** 7,899 cases.
- **Of those, containing "training":** 3,532 cases.
- **Percentage:** 3,532 / 7,899 = 44.71%, which rounds to 44.7%.

The 8 forms on the last pages of the April 2021 order use a checkbox layout, and my first pass missed them. Those forms list every option, and the first dollar amount printed is $5,000 on the training line. I counted all 8 as above $0 and containing "training". Without them the figure would be 3,524 / 7,891 = 44.66%, which also rounds to 44.7%.

I took each order's date from its file name, and all 58 fall within 2021–2025.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
**44.7%** of cases with a headline penalty above $0 mention "training" in the recommendation. That is 3,524 of 7,891 cases.

I parsed the recommendation text on every form page in the 58 order files. I took the first dollar amount, ignoring a doubled "$" and spaces after commas. I counted a case as mentioning training if the word appears anywhere in its recommendation, in any capitalization.

- **Total cases:** 9,963 forms across the 58 orders.
- **Zero-penalty cases:** 2,064 of the 9,963 have a headline penalty of $0 and are excluded from the denominator. Most are warning letters. Others are completed or already-done trainings, "Other", and one "no penalty recommended".
- **Unfilled forms:** a few forms came through as blank templates in the text. Their penalty fields are empty, so I counted them as $0. I couldn't check them against the PDFs, so if any held an amount in an image, the figure could shift slightly.
- **Penalty with no "$":** one case printed a bare "3000" with no "$". By the stated definition its headline penalty is $0, so I excluded it.
- **Nunc pro tunc order:** the April 2022 order (`ord_PSDNPT_040622`) corrects an address on one case. I counted its form as a case, with a $1,000 penalty and no mention of training.
```

## Q5-r3

arm `raw`, model `claude-sonnet-5-5`, wall 16.6 s

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Training option: among cases with a headline penalty above $0, what percentage have a recommendation that contains the word "training" (any capitalization)? Round to one decimal.
```

**System prompt:**

```
You are a careful analyst. You can use the Read, Grep, Glob and Bash tools (Bash has Python 3). Your working directory is the only place you may look for files; use only files in it. Show the number or answer each question asks for in your final reply.
```

```
Among cases with a headline penalty above $0, **44.7%** have a recommendation containing "training". That is 3,532 of 7,899 cases.

I parsed all 58 order text files. I took the text between "civil penalty" and "Abstentions" as the recommendation. I read the headline penalty as the first "$" amount, ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma. A recommendation like "Training or $5,000 if respondent fails to complete training" counts as $5,000 and contains "training".

The files hold 9,963 cases in total. The other 2,064 have a headline penalty of $0 and are left out. Every recommendation with a "$" sign yielded a parseable amount, so none were dropped.

I didn't check each order's date. I assumed all 58 files fall in 2021–2025, as the question states, and didn't filter by date.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
Estimate: **180 distinct normalized respondents** have 5 or more cases. Together they account for **24.1%** of non-utility-respondent cases (1,701 of 7,064). The 24.1% is an estimate only because of one case with a blank respondent, described below.

- **Cases parsed:** The 58 order files hold 9,963 recommendation forms. 186 of those are blank templates with no respondent, case number or violation, so I didn't count them as cases. That leaves 9,777 real cases.
- **Non-utility cases:** After removing cases whose respondent starts with one of the 13 utility names, 7,064 non-utility cases remain.
- **Blank respondent:** One real case (28073, in the 2023-02-08 order) has a blank respondent line in the text. I kept it in the 7,064 as a non-utility case. It can't affect the count of 180, since a single case can't reach 5. If you drop it, the percentage is still 24.1%.
- **Order dates:** I took all 58 orders as falling within 2021–2025 and did not check each order date.
- **Name matching:** The match is on the exact normalized names, so spelling variants of the same company, such as "Milenium", count as separate respondents.
```

## Q6-r2

arm `raw`, model `claude-sonnet-5-5`, wall 68.5 s

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Repeat concentration: take non-utility-respondent cases only. Normalize each respondent name, in this order: lowercase; delete every character that is not a letter, a digit or a space (no replacement); collapse runs of spaces to one and trim; then, once, drop a final whole word if it is exactly "inc", "llc", "co", "corp", "corporation" or "company". How many distinct normalized respondents have 5 or more cases, and what percentage of non-utility-respondent cases do they account for? Round the percentage to one decimal.
```

```
**187 distinct normalized respondents have 5 or more cases. Together they account for 24.3% of non-utility-respondent cases (1,760 of 7,243).**

- **Cases parsed:** I found 9,963 recommendation forms across the 58 order text files. Five of them (one each in the 03/03/21 and 04/06/22 orders, three in the 07/14/21 order) are blank template pages with no respondent, case number or cite. I excluded those, leaving 9,958 cases.
- **Parsing layouts:** Some orders print the field labels first and the values after them, and some respondent names wrap onto a second line. I handled both layouts.
- **Nunc pro tunc order:** The 04/06/22 nunc pro tunc order repeats case 24633, which is a CenterPoint (utility) case. It is a utility case, so it doesn't affect this answer.
- **Respondent with no name:** In the 02/08/23 order, case 28073 has a blank Respondent field and only Indiana Foundation Service, Inc. in the Other Party field. I counted that name as the respondent.
- **Cases left in:** 13 cases didn't match the "IC 8-1-26" cite pattern, probably from OCR errors. I kept them, since the question only needs the respondent name.
- **Estimate:** The count and percentage depend on reading some OCR-garbled names correctly, such as "N orthem" for "Northern". I believe the result is accurate, but a few names may be misread, so treat it as a close figure rather than an exact one.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
**187 distinct normalized respondents have 5 or more cases, and they account for 24.3% of non-utility-respondent cases.**

- **Cases read:** The 58 order files contain 9,963 recommendation forms.
- **Non-utility cases:** 7,243 cases are non-utility respondents.
- **Repeat respondents:** The 187 repeat respondents account for 1,760 of those cases, and 1,760 ÷ 7,243 = 24.3%.
- **Largest repeaters:** Miller Pipeline has 91 cases, Riethriley Construction Co has 54 (its name ends "Co Inc", so only the "inc" is dropped), and Calumet Civil Contractors has 48.

**Judgment calls on messy data:**
- **Wrapped names:** Some respondent names wrap onto a second line. I joined them before normalizing.
- **Scrambled forms:** In some orders the form's labels and values are printed in separate blocks. I matched them back up.
- **Blank forms:** Five forms (in orders 030321, 040622 and 071421) are completely blank, with no respondent or case number. I left them out of the case count.
- **Missing respondent:** One form (case 28073, order 020823) has a case number but no respondent name. I counted it as a non-utility case with an empty name. It falls in no group of 5 or more, so only the denominator is affected.
- **Correction order:** The nunc pro tunc order (PSDNPT_040622) restates case 24633 for CenterPoint, a utility, so it doesn't affect the count.

I did not check the order dates against the 2021–2025 window. I took all 58 files as given.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
Mean headline penalty per non-utility-respondent case, including $0 cases:

- **2021: about $2,375.** There are 1,227 non-utility cases and their headline penalties total $2,913,700, so the mean is $2,374.65.
- **2025: about $3,041.** There are 1,227 non-utility cases and their headline penalties total $3,731,000, so the mean is $3,040.75.

I computed these by parsing every case in the 2021 and 2025 order text files. Some form layouts and OCR errors needed special handling:

- **Blank forms:** I skipped blank template pages that had no respondent or case number.
- **Respondent names:** Some 2021 pages print the respondent and other party after the labels instead of beside them. For those I took the respondent as the entry two lines before the case number.
- **Checkbox forms:** Eight old-style checkbox forms in the April 2021 order (`ord_PSD_042121`) have no printed penalty line. I took the checked box instead. Seven are "Training or $5,000" and one is "$1,500". The Anderson Arbor Care form has garbled OCR, and I read it by eye as "Training or $5,000" and counted it as $5,000 by hand.
- **Misprinted case:** One 2021 case (Internattional Inc., case 0, `ord_PSD_020321`) prints its penalty as ".5000" with no "$". I counted it as $0, as the definition requires.
```

## Q7-r2

arm `raw`, model `claude-sonnet-5-5`, wall 52.4 s

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Expected cost per case: what is the mean headline penalty per case for non-utility-respondent cases in 2021, and in 2025? Include $0 cases. Round to the nearest dollar.
```


**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
Mean headline penalty per non-utility-respondent case, with $0 cases included:

- **2021: $2,368.** That is $2,912,200 across 1,230 cases, which works out to $2,367.64.
- **2025: $3,041.** That is $3,731,000 across 1,227 cases, which works out to $3,040.75.

I took the order year from the date in each filename. I read the penalty from the printed text and applied the "$$" and "$3, 500" rules.

Three judgment calls affect the 2021 figure:
- **Respondent names:** In one 2021 order (`ord_PSD_110321`), the OCR text put the respondent name on its own line instead of after "Respondent:". I read the name from there so those cases were classed correctly.
- **Checkbox forms:** Order `ord_PSD_042121` has checkbox-style forms. For these I used the checked option. One case (Pioneer Masonry) checks "Civil penalty of $1,500", so I counted $1,500. A literal first-dollar reading would give $5,000 there. That would add $3,500 to the 2021 total and change the mean by about $3.
- **Unreadable page:** One page in the 2021 order `ord_PSD_110321` had garbled OCR, so I dropped it from the count.
```

## Q7-r3

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Expected cost per case: what is the mean headline penalty per case for non-utility-respondent cases in 2021, and in 2025? Include $0 cases. Round to the nearest dollar.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
For non-utility-respondent cases, the mean headline penalty per case is **$2,375 in 2021** and **$3,041 in 2025**.

| Year | Non-utility cases | Total headline penalties | Mean per case |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2021 | 1,226 | $2,912,200 | $2,375.37, rounded to **$2,375** |
| 2025 | 1,227 | $3,731,000 | $3,040.75, rounded to **$3,041** |

Both means include the $0 cases (warning letters, "Training (completed)", and similar).

- **Blank forms:** I dropped four blank template pages (no respondent, cite or penalty) from the 2021 count. They aren't cases.
- **Scanned checkbox forms:** Eight cases in the April 2021 order are scanned checkbox forms. I took the checked option as the recommendation. Seven are "Training or $5,000", so they count as $5,000. One is a $1,500 civil penalty.
- **Garbled 2021 case:** One case in the February 2021 order has a garbled respondent ("130 2nd St NE") and a penalty printed as ".5000" with no "$". Under your rule it counts as $0.
- **Date from filename:** I took each order's year from the date in its filename, since the order pages carry no printed date. Only the nunc pro tunc order (a 2022 correction) shows a printed date, and its year agrees with its filename.
```

## Q8-r1

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Escalation: how many cases have a recommendation containing "ticket escalation" (any capitalization), and what is the most common headline penalty among those cases?
```

```
**446 cases** have a recommendation containing "ticket escalation", and the most common headline penalty among them is **$1,500**, which appears in 291 of the 446 cases.

I searched the text of all 58 orders, one recommendation form per page. I matched "ticket escalation" case-insensitively, but only in the committee's recommended penalty text. I counted a recommendation like "$3,000; no ticket escalation - additional $1,000" because it contains the phrase.

I read the headline penalty as the first dollar amount in each recommendation, using the rules you gave.

| Headline penalty | Cases |
|---|---|

| $3,000 | 56 |
| $5,000 | 47 |
| $3,500 | 23 |
| $4,000 | 15 |
| $4,500 | 11 |
| $1,000 | 2 |
| $6,000 | 1 |

These add up to 446, and no escalation case had a headline penalty of $0.

I counted by the filename dates (2021–2025) and did not check the date inside each order. One of the 58 files is a nunc pro tunc order, which I included because it is one of the 58.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
**446 cases** have a recommendation containing "ticket escalation", and the most common headline penalty among them is **$1,500**, which appears in 291 of the 446.

The next most common headline penalties are $3,000 (56 cases), $5,000 (47), $3,500 (23), $4,000 (15) and $4,500 (11).

- **Scope:** I read all 58 order text files and found 9,961 recommendation forms. I took each recommendation as the text after "The Advisory Committee recommends the following" up to the next "Abstentions" line or page break. I matched "ticket escalation" in any capitalization, including when a line break splits the phrase. The headline penalty is the first "$" amount, read under your rules for doubled "$" and spaces after commas.
- **Exclusion:** I left out 6 cases in the April 21, 2021 order. They use an older checkbox form, and the "No ticket escalation" box is printed but unchecked in all six. Counting them would give 452 cases, and $1,500 would still be the most common.
- **Count check:** The form count of 9,961 comes from my parser. A raw search for the recommendation phrase finds 9,962 lines, and I didn't track down that one-form difference. It is unlikely to change these figures.
- **Penalty cross-check:** The $1,500 forms use several wordings, such as "respondent can complete training instead of paying $1,500" and "$1,500; no ticket escalation-pay additional $1,000". I didn't reconcile the 291 against a separate hand count.
```

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Escalation: how many cases have a recommendation containing "ticket escalation" (any capitalization), and what is the most common headline penalty among those cases?
```

```
Among non-utility-respondent cases, the most common "other party" is **Northern Indiana Public Service Company**. It appears in **2,941 of 7,250** non-utility cases, which is **40.6%**.

- **Scope:** I parsed all 58 order files and found 9,963 recommendation forms. A 59th "Case Number" count of 9,965 in the text includes two references in the body of order `ord_PSDNPT_040622`, which I excluded. Of the 9,963 forms, 7,250 have a non-utility respondent.
- **Matching:** I matched the other-party name as exact text, ignoring case and surrounding spaces. I did not merge variant spellings. If you merged name variants such as "NIPSCO", the share could be higher.
- **Runners-up:** CenterPoint Energy Indiana is next with 2,427 cases (33.5%), then Citizens Gas with 902 (12.4%).
- **Blank names:** 213 non-utility cases have a blank other party. I kept them in the 7,250 denominator, and they don't change the top result.
```

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Who gets hit: among non-utility-respondent cases, which "other party" name appears most often (exact text, ignoring case and surrounding spaces), and in what percentage of non-utility-respondent cases? Round to one decimal.
```

```
Among non-utility-respondent cases, the other party that appears most often is **"Northern Indiana Public Service Company"**. It appears in **3,013 of 7,243 cases, or 41.6%**.

- **Data:** the 58 order text files contain 9,963 recommendation forms. I excluded 5 completely blank template forms, which have no respondent, other party or case number. That leaves 9,958 cases, of which 7,243 are non-utility respondents. One of those has a blank respondent field, and I counted it as non-utility.
- **Next in line:** CenterPoint Energy Indiana has 2,489 cases (34.4%). Citizens Gas has 922 (12.7%).
- **Counting rule:** I matched other-party names by exact text, ignoring case and surrounding spaces. Any OCR misspellings of the name would be counted separately. The top name is far enough ahead that this shouldn't change which one ranks first.
- **Missing other-party fields:** 32 non-utility cases have a blank other-party field, so they count in the denominator but never in a name's numerator.
```

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.
```

```
Expired tickets: among cases with base section 16(h), what percentage have the qualifier "expire", and what percentage have the qualifier "prior"? Round to one decimal.
```

```
Among cases with base section 16(h), **12.3%** have the qualifier "expire" and **6.0%** have "prior".

I parsed the cite from each of the 58 text-extracted orders. There are 3,932 cases with base section 16(h):

| Qualifier | Cases | Share of 3,932 |
|---|---|---|
| expire | 482 | 12.26%, so 12.3% |
| prior | 235 | 5.98%, so 6.0% |
| out | 180 | |
| other | 19 | |
```

```
Among cases with base section 16(h), **12.2%** have the qualifier "expire" and **6.0%** have "prior".

I counted only the cite printed directly under "finds the following violation(s)" in each of the 58 order text files. That gave 3,935 cases with base section 16(h):

| Qualifier | Cases | Share of 16(h) |
|---|---|---|
| expire | 482 | 482 / 3,935 = 12.25%, which rounds to 12.2% |
| prior | 235 | 235 / 3,935 = 5.97%, which rounds to 6.0% |
| out | 180 | not asked |
| other | 19 | not asked |
| none | 3,019 | not asked |

- **OCR misreads:** Three of the no-qualifier cases are OCR misreads of the cite, printed as "8-l-26-16(h)" or "IC8-l-26-16(h)". I counted them as 16(h).
- **Blank forms:** Six forms have an empty cite line. They are blank templates in the text, so I left them out of the counts. They are in ord_PSD_022322, ord_PSD_030321, ord_PSD_040622 and ord_PSD_071421 (three of them).

I did not check the order dates against the 2021–2025 range. I used all 58 text files, which matches the 58 orders you described.
```

```
**Indianapolis** has the most cases, with **1,153**. The five most common cities together account for **26.2%** of the cases that have a city.

- **Top five cities:** Indianapolis (1,153), Fort Wayne (530), Evansville (445), South Bend (295) and Bloomington (184). They total 2,607 cases.
- **Cases with a city:** 9,944. That is the 9,963 recommendation forms across the 58 orders, minus the ones that have no usable city.
- **Percentage:** 2,607 ÷ 9,944 = 26.2%.

Cases with no usable city:
- Nine have no damage location printed at all.
- Three have a location with no comma, such as "3846 Schneider Street" and "7401 ##".
- Several others have an empty city after the comma.

Two forms had a misaligned layout that my first extraction missed. I read their locations by hand: one is Fort Wayne and one is Geneva. Fort Wayne's count includes the Fort Wayne one.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
**Indianapolis** has the most cases, with **1,153**. The five most common cities together account for **26.2%** of the cases that have a city.

- **Top five cities:** Indianapolis (1,153), Fort Wayne (529), Evansville (445), South Bend (295) and Bloomington (184). Together that is 2,606 cases.
- **Base:** 9,942 cases have a city. I parsed 9,954 recommendation pages across the 58 orders and left out 12 for the reasons below.
- **Percentage:** 2,606 ÷ 9,942 = 26.21%, which rounds to 26.2%.

The 12 left out are:
- 6 pages whose damage location is just a comma.
- 3 pages with no comma at all.
- 3 pages where the comma is trailing or the page layout didn't parse (all in order 110321).

A few of these may be extraction problems in the text files rather than true blanks. Even if all 12 were included, the answer would barely move.

The case count is also not exact. A separate count of "Case Number" labels found 9,777, against 9,954 pages with a damage location. I didn't reconcile the two. I used pages because some forms put the labels and values in separate blocks. So the 9,942 base could be slightly off, but the ranking and the percentage should hold.
```

## H1-r3

arm `raw`, model `claude-sonnet-5-5`, wall 28.2 s

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Geography: define a case's **city** as the text after the last comma of its damage location, trimmed of spaces and lowercased, with spelling variants not merged; cases whose damage location has no comma or an empty city are left out of this question. Which city has the most cases, how many cases is that, and what percentage of the cases that have a city do the five most common cities together account for? Round the percentage to one decimal.
```

```
**Indianapolis** has the most cases, with **1,153**.

|---|---|---|

| 4 | south bend | 295 |
```

```
I parsed 9,953 cases from the 58 order text files. 11 had no comma or an empty city and were left out, which leaves the 9,942 above.

Some pages had the form labels and values split into separate blocks, so I re-paired them by position. Locations that wrapped onto a second line were joined before taking the text after the last comma. I did not check these by hand, so a few misread locations are possible. They would shift the counts slightly.
```

```
- **2023:** mean lag is **227 days** (227.3 unrounded, across 2,107 cases).
- **2024:** mean lag is **188 days** (187.7 unrounded, across 1,897 cases).
- **Lag over 365 days:** **12.6%** of all such cases. That is 9,955 cases across all years.

**Order date:** I took it from each order's file name (for example, `080923` is 2023-08-09, and `09.04.24` is 2024-09-04). The order text itself has no printed date.

**Which cases count:** A case counts if its form has a readable damage date in month/day/year form. The files hold 9,965 recommendation forms, and 10 were left out because their damage date is blank. Some forms print their values in a block after the labels instead of beside them. For those I read the damage date as the second date listed, after the advisory meeting date. No date failed to parse, and no lag came out negative.
```

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Reporting lag: for each case with a parseable damage date (month/day/year), define the **lag** as the number of calendar days from the damage date to the order date. Using the order's year (not the damage year), what is the mean lag in days for cases in 2023 and in 2024, each rounded to the nearest whole day, and what percentage of all such cases (all years) have a lag of more than 365 days? Round the percentage to one decimal.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
Mean reporting lag, using the order's year:

- **2023:** about **227 days**, over 2,107 cases (exact mean 227.34).
- **2024:** about **188 days**, over 1,897 cases (exact mean 187.67).
- **Lag over 365 days:** **12.6%** of all such cases, which is 1,258 of 9,956.

How I got these:

- **Order date:** The order text has no printed date, so I took it from each file name (for example, `ord_PSD_122023` is 12/20/2023).
- **Damage date:** I read it from each case's "Date of Damage" field. Some orders (such as `ord_PSD_110321`) print the labels and values in separate blocks, so there I took the last of the two dates in the case header. The first of the two is the advisory meeting date.
- **Excluded cases:** Six cases have a blank damage date and are left out. Their orders are dated 2021 and 2022, so they don't affect the 2023 and 2024 means.
- **Data check:** No case has a negative lag, and the shortest lag is 89 days.
```

```
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Reporting lag: for each case with a parseable damage date (month/day/year), define the **lag** as the number of calendar days from the damage date to the order date. Using the order's year (not the damage year), what is the mean lag in days for cases in 2023 and in 2024, each rounded to the nearest whole day, and what percentage of all such cases (all years) have a lag of more than 365 days? Round the percentage to one decimal.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
Estimated from the text files in the data folder. The parsing is not fully verified, but the checks I ran found no problems.

- **Mean lag, 2023 orders:** about **227 days**. The exact mean is 227.34 days over 2,107 cases.
- **Mean lag, 2024 orders:** about **188 days**. The exact mean is 187.67 days over 1,897 cases.
- **Share of cases with a lag over 365 days (all years):** **12.6%**. That is 1,258 of 9,956 cases with a parseable damage date.

I took the order date from each file name (for example, `ord_PSD_09.04.24` is 2024-09-04) and counted lag as calendar days from the damage date to that date. Six cases have a blank damage date and were left out. Some pages print the date before its label or on a separate line, so I read the damage date from those layouts too. No lag came out negative, and the shortest is 89 days.
```

```
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
```

```
- **Recorded abstentions:** the entries are names such as Smith, Warren, Miller, "Dickie & Smith" and Sontag. The only leaked-text case I found is one 2022 order, where "Dual Abstention" follows the leaked label, so it doesn't change these three years either.
```

```
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:

  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
Among cases with each qualifier, the share with a headline penalty of $0 is:

| Qualifier | Cases | Headline penalty $0 | Percentage |
|---|---|---|---|
| expire | 518 | 185 | **35.7%** |
| prior | 272 | 59 | **21.7%** |
| out | 191 | 35 | **18.3%** |

I parsed all 58 order text files and read the cite and the recommendation on each case page. A "Warning letter" recommendation has no "$" amount, so I counted it as $0.

One expire case (number 23444, in the 11/03/21 order) has OCR noise in the text, with "Com.m.ittee" in place of "Committee". My first parse missed it, so I added it by hand. It is a warning letter, so it counts as $0. That moves the expire result from 184 of 517 to 185 of 518. My counts of the prior and out cites match a raw search of the text files, and the expire count matches once that case is included.
```

```
Two caveats on data quality, neither of which affects these figures:
- **OCR noise:** the scanned text has some errors in the base sections, such as "8-l-26" and "lS(h)". The three qualifier words themselves were all clean.
```

## H4-r3

**Prompt** (from the .meta.json):

```
You are answering an actuarial question about Indiana's dig-damage enforcement record: the Indiana
Utility Regulatory Commission's orders approving the Underground Plant Protection Advisory Committee's
recommendations under Indiana Code chapter 8-1-26, for the 58 orders dated 2021-01-01 through 2025-12-31.
Definitions used in every question:
- **Case:** one recommendation form in an order (one case number on one page).
- **Year:** the year of the order's date, not the damage date, unless the question says otherwise.
- **Cite:** the IC 8-1-26 citation under "finds the following violation(s)". Every case has exactly one.
  **Base section:** the cite without "IC 8-1-26-" and without any parenthesized word. Example:
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
Give each number exactly as the question asks (rounding stated). If you cannot compute it from data you
have, say so and give your best estimate, labelled as an estimate.

Qualifier outcomes: a case has qualifier "expire", "prior" or "out" if its cite ends with that word in parentheses, on any base section. Among cases with each of those three qualifiers separately, what percentage have a headline penalty of $0? Round each to one decimal.
```

**Final answer** (verbatim):

```
Among cases with each qualifier, the share with a $0 headline penalty is:

| Qualifier | Cases | Headline penalty $0 | Percentage |
|---|---|---|---|
| expire | 517 | 184 | **35.6%** |
| prior | 271 | 58 | **21.4%** |
| out | 191 | 35 | **18.3%** |

I read these from the text files of all 58 orders, and I counted the "finds the following violation(s)" blocks to get the case totals.

- **Garbled cites:** The text extraction sometimes misread the cite, for example "8-l-26" in place of "8-1-26". I allowed for that.
- **Cites missing from the text:** Six cases have no cite in the text at all. I couldn't classify them, and I didn't check the PDFs. If any of them carries one of these three qualifiers, the counts above would be slightly off.
- **"Training or $5,000" cases:** Where a recommendation reads like "Training or $5,000 if respondent fails to complete training," the headline penalty is $5,000, because that is the first dollar amount printed.
- **Count mismatch:** I found 9,951 case blocks, but the text has only 9,965 "Case Number" lines and 7,825 "RecommendationForm" headers. The mismatch suggests the extraction is not perfectly clean, so treat the percentages as very close rather than certain.
```

```
  "IC 8-1-26-16(h)(expire)" has base section "16(h)" and qualifier "expire". A parenthesized word is
  two or more letters (hyphens allowed); single letters or digits in parentheses, like (h) or (a)(2), are
  part of the base section, so "20(a)(3)(b)" is its own base section, not "20(a)(2)".
- **Headline penalty:** the first dollar amount in the committee's recommendation, read as printed but
  ignoring a doubled "$" and any space after a thousands comma ("$$1000" is $1,000; "$3, 500" is $3,500).
  A recommendation with no "$" amount has a headline penalty of $0.
- **Utility respondent:** a case whose respondent name, ignoring case and leading spaces, starts with one of:
  "CenterPoint", "Northern Indiana Public Service", "NIPSCO", "Citizens Gas", "Citizens Energy",
  "Vectren", "Indiana American Water", "Ohio Valley Gas", "American Electric Power", "Duke Energy",
  "Community Natural Gas", "Midwest Natural Gas", "Frontier Communications". Every other case is a
  **non-utility respondent**.
```
